*Shenglong Yao,Zirui Liu,Xiangchi Yuan,Wenke Lee*

Abstract: Recent benchmarks show that LLMs achieve strong performance in both function-level binary understanding and end-to-end binary reverse engineering tasks, but whether LLMs can reason over interprocedural semantics in compiled binaries remains unclear. Function-level benchmarks offer limited visibility into cross-function relationships, while whole-program benchmarks typically evaluate final outcomes of tasks that may be solvable from local clues alone, making it difficult to disentangle interprocedural reasoning from exploration, tool use, execution, and retrieval. To address this limitation, we introduce ICALL-BENCH, a benchmark that directly evaluates whether LLMs and agents can understand and reason over interprocedural semantics in binary programs, using indirect-call target recovery as a measurable probe. ICALL-BENCH covers 50 complete real-world C/C++ programs spanning different complexity levels and multiple optimization configurations, with an average of 74,746 lines of code and 941 functions per program. We further introduce three analysis regimes spanning model-only reasoning, static tool-augmented agents, and Internet-enabled open-world agents to examine how intrinsic reasoning, active information acquisition, and external knowledge each contribute to interprocedural binary analysis. We evaluate five recent LLMs and their corresponding agents. Our results reveal a wide separation across current models and agents, with program-level verifiable success rates ranging from 23% to 81%. They also show that greater information access does not consistently lead to better results, with particularly limited gains on the hardest cases, where deeper interprocedural reasoning remains the primary bottleneck. Uneven performance across models and frequent verifiable failures even under an optimistic evaluation indicate that robust binary interprocedural reasoning remains an open challenge.

</details>

*Theocharis Tavantzis,Bianca Trinkenreich,Michaela Greiler,Júlia Condé Araújo,Marina Condé Araújo,Italo Santos,Alexander Serebrenik,Margaret-Anne Storey,Maria Teresa Baldassarre*

Abstract: Fast-paced AI-assisted software development risks reducing developers' understanding of the systems they build. This growing gap between what the system does and what developers understand about it, described as ``cognitive debt'', is not new, but AI has exacerbated it. This has led to a recent surge in blog posts and online discussions, in which practitioners recognize and write about cognitive debt, and share their experiences well before academic studies can capture them. To understand their concerns and inform future research and practice, we conduct a grey literature review of 76 sources, focusing on how cognitive debt is conceptualized and manifests, its contributing factors, its consequences, and the practices to prevent, detect, and mitigate it. We find that cognitive debt can accumulate unnoticed behind working code and passing tests, particularly when developers accept AI-generated solutions without building understanding. Practitioners report consequences such as harder maintenance and debugging, fragmented knowledge in the team, and reduced productivity. They propose practices such as documenting design rationale, review-sized changes, comprehension gates, and knowledge-sharing rituals, along with signals like reliability metrics to detect it. Our findings give software teams a structured view of emerging practical approaches in AI-assisted development and stress the need to preserve human understanding alongside faster code production.

</details>

Abstract: Self-adaptive systems are designed to accommodate variants of their environment, which form a family of instances whose uncertainty is captured by probabilistic models, and must meet probabilistic requirements on each instance. Within such a family, changes in size and structure complicate the fundamental adaptation decision: whether to retain, reuse, or construct a new controller, since controllers are often synthesized for and specific to an instance. Large language models (LLMs) have recently been used to write reusable controllers, but existing approaches largely target deterministic domains, and probabilistic model checking of their output remains limited to preliminary single-instance studies. In this paper, we propose a verifier-guided framework for controller reuse with explicit support for families of environments. The framework introduces a novel combination of LLM-written controllers with probabilistic model checking to provide per-instance guarantees for probabilistic requirements. We evaluate our approach using three LLMs on three benchmarks, assessing its effectiveness and cost with respect to baseline implementations, the distribution of adaptation decisions, and the impact of individual framework components. On grid worlds, LLM-written rule lists are verified on about 90% of held-out instances, against at most 77% for the baselines, and verifying a stored rule list is 15 to 103 times faster than per-instance synthesis. Reuse serves most episodes at a small fraction of the cost of generation or per-instance synthesis, and model-checking feedback increases verified coverage over independent restarts.

</details>

Abstract: Post-quantum cryptography (PQC) standards establish algorithms for quantum-safe systems, but empirical evidence about how software projects implement and maintain PQC support remains limited. We investigate this implementation and maintenance work by mining 114,361 commits and 5,713 issues across 179 open-source repositories. We use human annotation and validated LLM classification to identify PQC algorithms in commits, classify their adoption status and deployment mode, and identify technical challenges in issue discussions. The commit classification for PQC algorithms reveals that 90% of commits concern maintenance of existing support. From the issue discussions, we construct an 11-category taxonomy of recurring technical challenges. Build, packaging, and dependency failures, along with functional defects, account for approximately half of categorized issues. To examine how PQC support evolves, we use time-series analysis and first implementations of individual algorithms within repositories around NIST standardization milestones. After the 2024 finalization, standardized algorithm names appeared in more than 80% of the PQC development changes analyzed, although earlier names such as Kyber continued to appear. Development activity increased over the observation period. Our milestone analysis, however, did not find a significant increase in first implementations. Migration plans should therefore account for sustained maintenance, prioritize build reliability and integration testing, and track both standardized and predecessor algorithm names. We make the dataset, taxonomy, annotations, and analysis pipeline publicly available.

</details>

Abstract: A dynamic invariant detector proposes invariants, such as method pre- and postconditions, and rules out those that do not hold at run time. Traditional detectors propose invariants from a fixed library of templates, which limits their expressiveness. A large language model (LLM) can propose invariants instead of, or in addition to, template-derived ones. With the right prompt and context choices, we found that this idea can both improve the invariants' expressiveness and reduce the cost of inferring the invariants.
  Greater expressiveness brings complications: an LLM-proposed invariant can execute arbitrary code during invariant evaluation, causing side effects or infinite loops. Our novel instrumentation strategies mitigate these problems. We wrap each candidate and evaluate only one invariant per thread at a time, so nested invariant evaluations cannot trigger one another indefinitely. To recover from non-nested hangs, we monitor each run for stalls and retry it under a growing time budget. Because we cannot prove a candidate side-effect-free in advance, we use delta debugging over the invariants evaluated during a failing test to isolate and disable a side-effecting candidate without discarding the rest of the run.
  We compared our implementation, Oca, to the popular template-based Daikon invariant detector on real open-source Java projects. Oca produces more expressive invariants: on average, 28% of its held invariants call a project-specific method, which Daikon's templates cannot express. Its invariants are more often valid, with a false-positive rate of 18% in a manual inspection, vs. 76% for Daikon, and more useful: as properties for property-based testing, they exposed 9 of 25 real bugs, compared with 4 for Daikon. Oca also costs less, using 566 minutes and 27 GB of disk, compared with 2,817 minutes and 827 GB for Daikon.

</details>

Abstract: Generative AI (GenAI) makes open source software (OSS) contributions cheaper to produce, and it makes them say less about the people who produced them. In OSS mentoring, that matters: mentors judge what newcomers understand from their work, and newcomers learn from mentors' feedback. We studied how GenAI is changing mentoring in Google Summer of Code (GSoC), a program in which today's mentees can become tomorrow's mentors. We screened every thread on GSoC's restricted mentors' mailing list from November 2022 through March 2026 and interviewed 15 mentors and organization administrators. GenAI helped with onboarding, coordination, and first-pass review, but proposals, entry contributions, and mentees' answers became weaker evidence of what newcomers could do. Mentors made up the difference by checking, and that checking drew on the same time and goodwill the program needs to renew itself; one organization had withdrawn from GSoC. Responses ranged from banning GenAI to accepting it, and the boundary an organization drew determined who did the checking. We propose AIM, a decision framework that links acceptable GenAI use, integration into the mentoring pipeline, and mentoring capacity, to help organizations choose responses they can sustain.

</details>


### [7] [Skeleton Coding: Program Structure as a Specification for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38214)
*Aditya Golatkar,Alessandro Achille,Renos Zabounidis,Vlad Sobal,Yangruibo Ding,Baishakhi Ray,Jonathan Buck,Guy Van den Broeck,Maryam Fazel,Pietro Perona,Wei Xia,Stefano Soatto*

Abstract: Coding agents are typically provided end-to-end behavioral specifications that the code they produce should satisfy, such as tests and input--output examples. How many such specifications must a developer provide, and which ones, for the agent to produce the developer's intended program? We argue that this cannot be determined a priori, for it depends on the structure of the implementation, which is left to the agent to decide. An agent first commits to a structure---a decomposition into data types, modules, and functions---which fixes a class of candidate programs; behavioral specifications then select a program within that class. A set of tests that suffices for one structure may leave another badly underdetermined, requiring different edge cases or exponentially more examples. Since the structure is chosen by the agent, developers cannot ensure their specification is complete without reverse-engineering it from thousands of lines of generated code. To resolve this, we introduce Skeleton Coding, in which developers jointly specify the structure---in high-level, code-like format---alongside behavioral specifications. Theoretically, we model AI-assisted coding as a control problem, and show that skeletons can reduce exponentially the number of tests developers must supply to steer an agent toward an acceptable program. Empirically, across four models and three benchmarks, the best-performing skeleton specifications reduce mean test error by 48.4% relative to the best end-to-end behavioral specifications. Even when the agent can query a complete reference implementation, adding a skeleton reduces mean test error relatively by up to 27.0%. Skeletons also narrow the average gap between small and large models by about 52.4% while lowering implementation cost by 15--26x, and reduce code erosion by 24.7% on average.

</details>

Abstract: Existing code reveals past implementation decisions but rarely explains the reasoning behind them. When coding agents implement new features or modify existing functionality, past decisions may conflict with new task instructions. This raises a fundamental question for agentic software evolution: when multiple implementations satisfy the same functional requirements, how do coding agents decide whether to follow existing code or new task instructions? We investigate this question through bidirectional, controlled experiments in purpose-built repositories, followed by validation in real-world open-source repositories. Our approach isolates three competing sources of guidance: (1) agents' default preferences, (2) repository precedents, and (3) explicit task instructions. We conduct 5,625 trials across four coding agents and six implementation pairs, with validation in four open-source repositories spanning three programming languages. We find that agents share the same default implementation choices, yet overwhelmingly follow repository precedent when it conflicts with those defaults (359/360 trials). Crucially, not all precedents carry equal influence: three supporting local files yield 96% adoption, compared with only 4% for sibling-resource files, and changing file placement alone can shift adoption from 0% to 100% at a fixed example count. When new task intent conflicts with existing code, written priority rules achieve substantially higher success than code examples alone (59/60 versus 38/60 joint successes for two agents on two implementation pairs). The effectiveness of explicit rules also extends to open-source repositories, where a harness-loaded rule raises joint success from 0/120 to 117/120. These findings motivate design-decision traceability that preserves not only implementation choices but also the rationale and priorities that should guide future changes.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-generated explanations help developers understand automated program repair (APR) rationale and assess patch quality. Incorrect explanations can mislead developers about a repair's reliability, making their detection important. However, manually inspecting the growing volume of generated repairs is impractical, motivating automated detection.
  Existing methods mainly target incorrect answers from general question-answering LLMs, often by sampling multiple responses and detecting contradictions. Applied to APR, they face two limitations: (1) they may depend on external verifiers that are unavailable or lack APR-specific reasoning; and (2) sampling strategies such as paraphrasing or re-prompting the same input often yield superficial, redundant responses that repeat the original error rather than expose it.
  We propose MetaReJ, the first method designed to detect incorrect APR explanations using metamorphic testing. Inspired by debate and viva examinations, MetaReJ challenges explanations through an argumentative strategy without requiring external verifiers. It applies novel metamorphic relations (MRs) to recursively question the LLM under equivalent and non-equivalent repair scenarios and detect contradictions. To obtain diverse samples, MetaReJ transforms the buggy program into variants that preserve or alter the underlying bug, then asks the LLM how to repair each variant. Contradictions among responses are encoded as MR violations, which indicate that the original explanation is incorrect. We evaluate MetaReJ on a manually labelled dataset of 1,756 explanations from multiple LLM-based APR systems. MetaReJ achieves an MCC of up to 0.43, outperforming prior methods by up to 333.3% in MCC.

</details>

Abstract: A range of software engineering tasks, such as optimization and refactoring, seek to preserve the behavior of software components and require methods to assess functional similarity. Research has explored formal approaches to prove functional equivalence and used machine learning methods to approximate it, but the former does not scale and does not quantify the extent of similarity and the latter struggles when subtle semantic differences are present. Differential fuzzing offers a scalable approach for differencing programs semantically, but applying it often requires substantial manual effort to construct input generators that are well-suited to a target pair of programs. To address this challenge, we introduce DIFUZZI, which incorporates an LLM-based generator synthesis step that is iteratively validated and repaired. By automating this step, DIFUZZI paves the way for broader applicability of differential fuzzing. We release DIFUZZI at https://github.com/Dristi123/difuzzi_tool, with a demonstration video at https://youtu.be/FlHNOHuo9WE

</details>

Abstract: Coding agents automatically load project memory files such as CLAUDE.md at the start of every session and treat them as trusted project guidance. Anyone who can change such a file, for example through a pull request, can therefore steer the agent without ever touching the developer's prompt. We study memory files as an attack surface for insecure code generation and ask (1) whether a malicious memory file makes agents produce code that passes its tests but is vulnerable, and (2) whether agents disclose to the developer that the file shaped their code. For each of 12 CWE classes, we crafted two malicious memory files, a qualified variant that presents removing defensive checks as engineering best practice and a forceful variant that demands it. We ran Claude Code (Sonnet 5.5) and OpenCode (Qwen 3.8 27B, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash) on 36 SecRepoBench tasks with and without these files. The malicious files changed the rate of secure completions by -8 to +6 percentage points, with no consistent pattern across agents or variants. Agents differ sharply in what they tell the developer about the file. Claude Code names it in about two thirds of its final messages, whereas OpenCode mentions it almost exclusively in its reasoning trace, not in the final message the developer reads. Flagging the file as an attack does not reliably protect the code, as such agents still generated vulnerable code. We outline the larger studies needed to quantify this risk across agents and to design defenses.

</details>

Abstract: When an LLM agent fails, repair methods choose which action to replace and what to put in its place. They often score candidate repairs with greedy replay, a stronger model, an environment oracle, or a judge that reads the failed log. We ask whether these evaluators pick repairs that help the agent that will actually execute them. Holding failed trajectories and candidate single-step repairs fixed, we label every candidate with independent rollouts of the deployed policy and measure how much deployment value each evaluator's choice loses. On failures not screened for repair value, pre-specified tests show that greedy replay loses 0.08-0.13 in expected net success gain in ALFWorld and 0.06-0.13 in net reward in WebShop, relative to selection with the deployed agent's own rollouts. At an equal rollout budget, selecting repairs for Qwen2.5-7B with Qwen2.5-14B rollouts loses 0.25 in ALFWorld and 0.07 in WebShop, twenty 14B rollouts per candidate are worth no more than two of the 7B agent's own, and fresh rollouts confirm that the two models prefer opposite repairs on selected disagreement cases. Between similar-sized models from different families, the losses appear in both directions in both environments (0.07-0.17). The scoring executor also changes repair-location labels and method rankings. Repairs should be verified with the executor that will run them, and repair labels should name their executor.

</details>

Abstract: Smart contracts are immutable programs deployed on blockchain platforms, where vulnerabilities can translate directly into financial loss. Among the techniques proposed for their detection, recent work has relied increasingly on Large Language Models (LLMs) guided by carefully crafted prompts. However, the evidence accumulated so far rests almost entirely on the dense Transformer architecture behind the GPT line and on model snapshots already scheduled for deprecation, leaving open the question of whether the reported behaviors reflect a general property of LLMs or an artifact of that specific design.
  This paper presents an extended replication of the empirical study by Chen et al. on LLM-based smart contract vulnerability detection. We replicate the original protocol on GPT-4 as a baseline, followed by a differentiated replication, executing the protocol on representative models based on Mixture-of-Experts and RNN-like designs, together with an attempted preliminary evaluation of State Space Models. The exact replication confirms the findings of Chen et al., including the high-recall, low-precision detection pattern they report on GPT-4. Nevertheless, the differentiated replication shows that this pattern does not transfer uniformly across the evaluated models. Those from the same architectural family may also exhibit different behaviors, indicating that architectural family alone is insufficient to predict the resulting detection profile. We observe the same qualitative dense-Transformer profile in both GPT-4 and Gemma-3, despite a gap of roughly two orders of magnitude in parameter counts, whereas two Mixture-of-Experts models from different lineages exhibit qualitatively divergent behaviors, indicating that architectural family alone is insufficient to predict the detection profile.

</details>

Abstract: Simulation is essential for testing automated driving systems (ADS), but discrepancies between simulation and reality can undermine testing conclusions. It remains unclear which discrepancies the literature addresses and what evidence supports correspondence claims. We present a systematic mapping of 235 primary studies that measure or mitigate the reality gap in ADS testing, selected from 4,195 publications. We characterise objectives, targets, approaches, experimental setups, and evaluation evidence. Our classification distinguishes simulated and real-world execution across six ADS test components, separately for each approach and its evaluation reference. This distinguishes real-world information used to construct an approach from evidence used to evaluate its claims. Of the included studies, 76% address mitigation, primarily targeting scenario and actor realism and perception, while 94% report validation or measurement procedures. Only 61 studies report metrics from multiple families covering sensor/perception correspondence, ADS behaviour, vehicle dynamics, or safety outcomes; this coverage does not establish causal links between discrepancies and test verdicts. The synthesis highlights that real-world content and physical execution must be interpreted against the claim evaluated. The findings motivate claim-specific evaluation and component-level reporting to establish which discrepancies affect testing conclusions and when simulation evidence supports conclusions about real-world ADS behaviour.

</details>

Abstract: Monitoring and debugging deployed software is crucial for scalability, performance, and robustness. Cross-cutting concerns are pervasive across software ecosystems, yet engineers must address them with a fragmented toolbox. While high-level frameworks exist to offer application-level types and semantics, they often lock a solution into specific languages, runtimes, or hardware architectures. Portability often forces engineers to choose lower-level instrumentation mechanisms. For example, in-language frameworks can trace a request across processes, but multi-language contexts require instrumentation at the boundary, for example using eBPF. While these frameworks provide powerful capabilities, they often result in 1) a tradeoff between application-level types or low-level bytes and packets, 2) non-composable domain-specific tools, 3) feature fragmentation across frameworks, and 4) tools that often run with unrestricted access to the program and its environment.
  We address these problems with Splicer, a WebAssembly component interposition framework that 1) provides semantically rich application-level types, 2) enables pluggable tooling with powerful capabilities, 3) reduces ecosystem fragmentation through porting across languages and hardware, and 4) confines each tool to a sandbox with explicitly granted permissions. We evaluate Splicer on a suite of benchmarks demonstrating the pluggability of non-trivial tooling written in multiple source languages and their performance.

</details>

Abstract: AI coding agents increasingly rely on retrieved code and development history to guide software changes. However, highly relevant artifacts may describe implementations that are inactive or incompatible with the target deployment. Neither semantic similarity nor recency establishes whether retrieved information remains applicable under feature flags, architectural migrations, and deployment-specific configurations. We challenge relevance-oriented retrieval by introducing operational context validity, a task-, deployment-, and time-dependent criterion for determining whether software evidence can legitimately guide implementation decisions. We instantiate this new retrieval principle in CASE, an agent-agnostic framework that connects repository artifacts with runtime configurations and architectural evolution, classifies evidence as valid, invalid, or unknown, and generates context packages distinguishing actionable guidance from historical rationale and unresolved constraints. We investigate its feasibility through a reproducible preliminary evaluation involving six development tasks, 15 artifacts, and three deployments. Conventional retrieval exposes 16 incompatible artifact occurrences as actionable, whereas CASE excludes them while retaining all applicable artifacts under the prototype's reference rules. Recency filtering reduces valid recall to 0.43, while removing feature-flag evidence from CASE reduces it to 0.13. Although these results demonstrate feasibility rather than improvements in coding-agent correctness, they reveal limitations of relevance-based retrieval and motivate a new research direction: enabling coding agents to reason not only about which information is relevant, but also whether it remains operationally valid.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models pass most functional-correctness benchmarks, but passing tests says little about whether their code is readable, efficient or secure. Many techniques claim to improve these qualities, yet their evidence is scattered, usually covers one quality dimension and is rarely compared at an equal compute budget. We contribute a systematic review and a controlled re-evaluation. A systematic literature review of 628 primary studies (July 2021 to October 2026), with disclosed, author-reviewed LLM assistance, organizes the techniques by stage, signal source and quality target. Most studies measure only the dimension they target, gains often come with unchanged or unreported correctness, single automated quality signals are gamed, and compute, variance and contamination are rarely reported. A controlled re-evaluation runs ten techniques on four models at matched budgets on correctness, maintainability, efficiency and security benchmarks, extends it to thirteen current models and repository-level tasks, and compares 66 highlighted techniques head-to-head on Gemma 4 31B. At one call, quality prompting raises analyzer issue counts and leaves complexity and judge scores almost unchanged. At matched budgets, best-of-n sampling with an analyzer-weighted ranker matches or beats prompting, planning, self-refinement, a reviewer agent and retrieval on measures the ranker does not optimize, while an analyzer feedback loop and fine-tuning beat it only on analyzer issue counts. Large correctness losses on HumanEval+, a likely contaminated benchmark near its ceiling, do not appear on contests held after the models' training cutoffs. In the head-to-head, 1 of 66 techniques beats best-of-n on its primary target beyond the 95% interval, a rate consistent with chance. Gains should be reported against a budget-matched sampling baseline and on measures independent of the technique's own signal.

</details>

One library's timeout throws an exception no caller can catch, ending a Node.js process. A library can conform under jsdom and deviate in every tested Chrome- and Safari-family browser.

Abstract: Empirical studies of polyglot repositories apply the same analyses to every language of a corpus, such as separating code from comments, computing metrics, normalizing identifiers, and screening for duplicated code. Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on software engineering (SE) tasks, driving the need for high-quality datasets for reliable training and evaluation. SWE-bench-style datasets (SWE datasets) are widely used to evaluate LLM-based coding agents on real-world software issues, where task clarity and test coverage directly affect whether instances are solvable and verifiable. However, quality issues persist in existing SWE datasets, and manual annotation is costly and labor-intensive, motivating LLM-based annotation. Existing approaches typically rely on static, overly general rubrics, limiting their ability to handle diverse and nuanced scenarios and often yielding high false positive rates. In contrast, human annotators draw on accumulated experience to make context-aware judgments. Inspired by this, we propose ExLabel, an LLM-based, self-evolving, experience-guided assistant for task clarity and test coverage assessment in SWE datasets. ExLabel maintains an evolving experience bank that distills cross-instance knowledge into stable yet adaptive judgment criteria and reuses relevant experience to guide the assessment of new instances. For systematic evaluation, we construct SWE-TQ, a new benchmark spanning eight programming languages, by cleaning human annotations from SWE-bench and Multi-SWE-bench. Experiments with three backbone LLMs show that ExLabel achieves the best F1 across all settings, with relative improvements of 44.2%/49.9% and 24.0%/18.4% over the best baseline for task clarity and test coverage assessment on the Python/Multilingual subsets, respectively. Moreover, a coding model fine-tuned on SWE-rebench data filtered by ExLabel resolves 66.2% of issues on SWE-bench Verified, outperforming the model trained on data filtered by the best baseline by 14.5%.

Abstract: AI coding agents now do long, multi-step and multi-session software work with little supervision. When a developer comes back to that work, the useful question is not what the model said but what was actually done, by whom, and whether it is finished. We study this in three parts. First, we verify Agenttrail, an open-source local observability tool for coding agents, against its source at commit e4ba2da: nine documented constants and mechanisms hold at file-and-line level, two event pipelines are never unified, and its Map service has an unauthenticated local mutation surface. Second, we run a prototype of our system on 45 real coding-agent sessions (102,233 records, 20,577 tool executions, 2,336 human-to-agent episodes). With a blind-audited classifier (precision 83.3% and 96.7%) we estimate that 23.4% (95% CI 17.1 to 35.7) of code-editing episodes end with no independent check after the last edit, and that 57.8% of to-dos the agent marked complete after editing had no proof in between. A 10-minute timing heuristic for session hand-offs recovers 6 of the 24 session pairs that content lineage links. 42% of sessions run longer than 24 hours, 48.1% of transcript bytes lie past a 2 MiB replay bound, and 1,326 records in 16 sessions contain credential-like strings. Third, we propose Soei (Structured Observability of Evidence and Intent): an acknowledgement-aware event schema, a hash-chained local ledger, a W3C PROV-aligned graph, calibrated inference for attribution and lineage, and a verification layer that keeps a claim of completion apart from proof. On the same corpus the ledger is 20% of raw size, holds no content or credentials, verifies at about 126k events/s, detects tampering and replays deterministically. We position the work against concurrent 2026 proposals and define autonomy levels with evidence gates and harness metrics baselined on our data.

</details>

Abstract: Code language models complete, review, and repair programs without exposing the program structure encoded in their hidden states. In this paper, we test whether the hidden states of a code model can be described and edited using the information a compiler front end computes. For each token of a Python program, standard analyzers provide its position in the abstract syntax tree, its scope, its definition-use links, and its syntactic category. We fit a decomposition that expresses every hidden state of a frozen model as a sum of one term per token, each combining the token with this structural label, and we replace the model's own states with the decomposition at every layer while it predicts the output of a small program. If the model still answers correctly, the decomposition preserves the information the model uses. We apply this test to 14 open models, seven of them trained for code, and compare against a baseline decomposition of the same size built from token positions. The static-analysis decomposition preserves the model's answers better than the positional one in 10 of the 14 models, including six of the seven code models, and deleting the definition-use links from it removes most of that advantage on the two models tested. Editing the decomposition, for instance rebinding the argument of a call, makes StarCoder2-15B produce the answer of the edited program in 90 of 100 cases, compared with 19 for the positional baseline. The Qwen2.5 family is the exception on several measures. For most of the models tested, standard static analysis therefore provides both a description of what the hidden states encode about a program and a way to change it without retraining.

</details>

Abstract: Code embedding models have become a key building block for many software engineering applications, yet their internal representations remain poorly understood. Existing evaluations tend to focus on downstream task performance, offering limited insight into whether a single embedding can support both functional abstraction and implementation-sensitive analysis. To address this gap, we present a large-scale empirical study of 14 state-of-the-art code embedding models. We evaluate their representational behavior through three complementary analyses: supervised probing for functional abstraction and implementation fidelity, zero-shot retrieval experiments to assess geometric similarity, and controlled perturbation experiments that measure embedding responses to semantics-preserving code transformations. The results reveal a consistent abstraction-fidelity tension: models that better preserve stylistic and structural signals tend to do so at the expense of semantic generalization, while models with similar retrieval performance can nevertheless respond very differently to the same implementation changes. Overall, our findings suggest that a single universal code representation remains out of reach, motivating more representation-aware evaluation, selection, and deployment of code embedding models in software engineering.

</details>

Abstract: Microservice architectures aim to localize change, yet feature requests may require implementation across service boundaries. We investigate how well such downstream scope can be anticipated before implementation begins. Using issue--pull-request--commit traceability and implementation evidence corrected through targeted human adjudication, we reconstruct 866 repository-traceable feature requests from 13 open-source microservice projects and map their implementations to validated service boundaries. Cross-service changes occur in 101 requests (11.7%) and exhibit broader implementation footprints, particularly higher code churn, but no clear difference in lead time. Pre-implementation evidence provides limited cross-project predictive signal: the strongest leave-one-system-out model reaches a project-macro Macro-F1 of 0.484, but its advantage over the majority baseline is not significant after Holm correction and is sensitive to small evaluation folds. A local LLM offers no reliable improvement over classical models. Downstream workload is substantially harder to estimate, as learned models do not consistently outperform training-fold median baselines. A simple service-mention heuristic also performs comparably to learned scope triage. These findings show that early feature-request evidence is more useful for lightweight cross-service scope triage than for automated scope determination or precise workload estimation.

</details>

Abstract: Research on requirements-to-code tasks needs examples in which a genuine requirement is paired with the change that implemented it. Existing user-story collections, however, largely contain isolated or synthetic text. To address this limitation, we present STORY2DIFF, a dataset of 7,785 English user stories linked to merged pull requests from January 2017 to July 2026. The dataset is mined from a set of 982 public Java and Python repositories. Each record in the dataset combines the original issue, extracted Connextra elements, the associated pull request and commit metadata, a file-level diff capturing the code changes introduced by the associated pull request, and the affected files before and after the change. The tool we built to mine STORY2DIFF can be used to repeat the automatic extraction of the records, using different parameters (e.g., a different timeframe). The dataset can support a variety of tasks, including requirement discovery, traceability, change- impact estimation, and requirements-aware code generation. Abstract: Automated vulnerability repair must often operate with incomplete vulnerability information: developers may lack an accurate vulnerability classification or a provided proof-of-vulnerability (PoV) test, yet still need to produce a repair. Without detailed information, a key challenge is to explore multiple plausible interpretations of the vulnerability before committing to a repair. To address this challenge, we propose ExploreRepair, a vulnerability repair framework that reconciles the vulnerability observations and repair implementations from a team of exploration agents. We first collect these observations and repair implementations from three agents, each tasked with exploring a specific dimension of the vulnerability. Then, a Lesson Aggregator and a Patch Aggregator independently synthesize repairs from these observations and concrete implementations before a final Aggregator reconciles their proposals without benchmark feedback. On PatchEval, ExploreRepair achieves repair success rates of 39.6% and 71.6% with GPT-5.6-Luna and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, respectively, exceeding EvoRepair, a state-of-the-art vulnerability repair approach, by 16.0 and 25.4 percentage points. On VulnLoc+, it achieves success rates of 51.4% and 62.9%, respectively, exceeding LoopRepair's variant without PoV tests by 20.0 and 17.1 percentage points.

Abstract: Neuron coverage criteria aim to assess test suite quality and guide test input generation, but their effectiveness for Transformer-based language models remains unclear. We evaluate six criteria, including four established and two recent criteria, across encoder-only, decoder-only, and encoder-decoder models. Our evaluation examines error-revealing capability, distribution diversity, sample fairness (category diversity), and guidance for test input generation. The results reveal uneven effectiveness. Erroneous samples provide only 0.32% additional coverage over noise samples, indicating limited sensitivity to adversarial errors. By contrast, coverage criteria effectively assess distribution diversity and sample fairness in 26 and 30 of 33 configurations, respectively. Incorporating neuron-wise coverage guidance into test input generation increases adversarial sample production by only 4.56%. These findings suggest that existing coverage criteria can serve as an antidote for assessing diversity but remain largely a placebo for revealing adversarial errors, particularly when used individually. Their practical value therefore depends on whether these limited benefits justify the computational overhead of testing large Transformer-based models. Our study highlights the need for more robust and efficient coverage criteria tailored to these models, with stronger error-revealing capability and greater value for test generation.

Abstract: To succeed in real-world long-horizon software development, coding agents must satisfy multi-dimensional task specifications, such as interface compatibility and error handling, and do so without breaking existing dependencies. However, current works lack specification in both quantity and diversity, often emphasizing primary goals while ignoring complex constraints. Synthesizing tasks to bridge this gap presents two challenges: naively adding specifications risks reducing tasks to trivial checklists rather than compelling joint reasoning, and verifying these subtle interactions risks overlooking hidden flaws or rejecting valid alternatives. To address these challenges, we introduce CodeSpecWeave, a framework for synthesizing tasks with rich, interdependent specifications. Grounded in real developer seeds, a synthesis agent iteratively develops both coupled task requirements and executable tests, while a review agent audits solution trajectories from multiple models to guarantee test reliability. Using this pipeline, we construct 4,670 training tasks across 2,865 repositories. Applying Reinforcement learning on these tasks significantly improves performance over the base model on DeepSWE-v1.1, largely outperforming existing baselines. Furthermore, we curate CodeSpecWeave-Bench, a rigorous benchmark comprising 100 tasks and 1,044 specifications, which clearly separates frontier models and reveals significant headroom in handling interdependent engineering constraints.

</details>

Abstract: Pasting a legacy file into an LLM and asking it to "modernize this" is an easy shortcut. But is the result better than leaving the code alone? In this paper, we study this single-file setting with five LLMs, each asked to modernize 100 WordPress 4.0 PHP files to PHP 8.3 while preserving behavior, with no project context or execution feedback. We evaluate every output with 630 hidden behavioral checks and 511 Rector-detected modernization findings. We compare against two baselines: the unchanged originals and the rule-based tool Rector. Often, the answer is no. Untouched, 83 files already pass every check; the best LLM leaves only 59. Each model breaks 32-51 working files while repairing at most 10, whereas Rector breaks 7 and repairs 6. Most of this damage is unnecessary. Only 37 findings (7 percent) require a change under PHP 8.3, yet in compatible files with no required change, the LLMs break 36-60 percent (Rector: 8 percent). The model with the highest modernization score is no more compatible than its closest competitors. Most regressed checks come from files that no longer load at all, often because of type declarations PHP itself rejects. These are errors that need no project context to avoid. Evaluations of LLM-based modernization should therefore measure against a no-change baseline and report modernization and behavioral preservation separately.

Abstract: Challenges and chances of mathematical software are directly entangled with the programming language used. Depending on the background of developers and users, conflicts between different goals may arise. While several, e.g., scripting languages allow a low barrier and user-friendly entry to mathematical modeling and simulation, corresponding implementations will most likely not allow timely outcomes for detailed simulations.
  In infectious disease modeling, while several large-scale software frameworks rely on C or C++ implementations, most models are implemented in Python or R. While the latter allow for fast prototyping and more interactive development, it has been shown for several applications that C or C++ implementations outperform plain Python or R implementations by factors between one and three orders of magnitude. In particular, in times of pandemics, the urgent need for swift estimation of the system's dynamics makes the use of large-scale models in these noncompiled languages prohibitive.
  In order to create synergies and close the gap between scientists from the application and the computational domain, binding libraries allow to execute entire, compiled software frameworks from languages such as Python. However, with iterative model development, extension, or addition of new models, implementing bindings can represent another relevant barrier to developers of the C or C++ backends. In this paper, we show how a large-scale software framework with a multitude of infectious disease models can be efficiently exposed to Python. In addition, we suggest a semi-automated way to generate interface code by analyzing generic C++ code through abstract syntax trees, enabling a modularized approach working with intermediate representations.

</details>

Abstract: Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models offer scalable capacity for Web applications, but uneven routing creates computation and communication bottlenecks. Existing placement and replication methods redistribute this workload, yet additional expert copies compete with token-related buffers for limited GPU memory. We present LRMoE, a memory-aware MoE training system that jointly recalibrates backward demand and balances the retained workload. During forward execution, it plans retention quotas and expert placement under resource constraints while preserving all routed computations. During backpropagation, a load recalibration metric estimates each token-expert pair's contribution to the expert update and selects pairs within these quotas. A capacity-aware routing algorithm distributes retained pairs among eligible instances of their original experts. Physical compaction then forms smaller communication payloads and variable-size grouped GEMMs, reducing backward traffic, computation, and execution-buffer demand. Experiments on Qwen3-30B-A3B and GLM4.5-106B-A12B show that LRMoE reduces cumulative MoE-layer training time to fixed-loss targets by 21.2-44.8% relative to SYMI.

Abstract: Model discrepancy may prevent a single calibration parameter from reproducing a physical response across the context domain. In addition, uncertainties in observations and contexts affect calibration and must be accounted for and quantified. We construct a family of localized posteriors by weighting observation likelihoods according to their proximity to a target context. Gaussian localization weights incorporate context uncertainty through covariance inflation, with reflection at domain boundaries and an optional correction for uneven context sampling. A conditional normalizing flow approximates the family of posteriors through amortized variational inference. Integrating the localized posteriors under a prescribed context measure gives the marginalized parameter distribution. In a twin experiment with the Branin-Hoo model, we compare global and localized inference under uniform and skewed context distributions. Localization reduces the reported aggregate predictive errors relative to the global posterior. An application to the k-$ε$ turbulence closure in a single-column ocean model, with atmospheric surface forcing as context, shows that localized posterior calibration reduces temperature and zonal-velocity errors relative to deterministic and global-posterior calibration. Both twin experiments are constructed to admit local compensation.

Abstract: Many uses of language models end in a typed decision: a choice among a closed set of options, whose probabilities code can act on. Reading those probabilities from one forward pass is fast but often wrong on decisions that take several steps, such as arithmetic, counting, and date comparison. Letting the model think first fixes most of these errors, but costs seconds per decision instead of milliseconds. To pay for thinking only where it helps, ThinkJev builds a fast and a slow system from one open hybrid-thinking model that can answer directly or after a thought. System 1 reads the model's probabilities for the option letters when it answers directly; System 2 reads the same letters after the model has thought on the same cached prompt, so escalating repeats no work and both systems give probabilities over the same options. Because the letters can be read after any number of thought tokens, one sampled thought also gives the answer under every thinking budget. Without training, ThinkJev lets System 1 answer alone only where a statistical test on labeled dev data guarantees that its accepted answers stay under a target error rate. At a 5% target, Qwen3.5-27B answers 24.5% of 8359 test decisions this way with a realized error of 3.9% (Qwen3.5-4B and 9B: 6.5% and 0.0%). For the rest, stopping each thought as soon as its answer is confident reaches 81.6% accuracy, against 82.7% for always thinking, with 72% of the thought tokens. Choosing when to think from System 1's confidence alone saves less, because the decisions System 1 is least sure of are often not the ones thinking fixes. On the public items of JevBench, an independent benchmark of typed-decision services, Qwen3.5-27B thinks on 29.4% and is not distinguishable in accuracy from the two thinking APIs listed (exploratory). Code and data generators are available at https://github.com/ding-xiaolu/ThinkJev.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown increasing promise in research-level problem solving, yet a comprehensive collection of such problems is still missing, and evaluating them on research-level problems remains challenging. We introduce OpenFrontiers, a cross-disciplinary resource that combines a structured corpus of open research problems with a retrospective benchmark of recently resolved problems in mathematics. The corpus records problem statements, context, progress, and other information for 12,151 problems. The benchmark contains 200 problems, 110 resolved in 2026 and 90 still open, each with a Lean~4 statement checked by automated probes and two independent reviews; a subset HQ-50 further filters based on quality score, etc. Our evaluation protocol grades formal proofs with two independent checkers in an isolated sandbox under closed-book, frozen-retrieval, and live-web conditions. Evaluating seven LLM agents on the HQ-50 subset under a standardized budget, we find that closed-book rediscovery is rare, at most 7 of 40 resolved problems, whereas live-web success mostly reflects retrieval of published solutions. By linking a broad research-problem corpus to a formally graded evaluation subset with documented solutions, OpenFrontiers provides a foundation for measuring research-level rediscovery and studying progress toward solving currently open scientific problems. Abstract: LLM agents that answer questions over Web knowledge graphs are increasingly wrapped in checks derived from the graph's ontology, on the assumption that a formal schema makes a reliable judge. Yet a checker can be wrong as well: it can miss errors, and it can flag correct answers and push the agent into "fixing" them. Whether a curated ontology is worth its cost as such a judge, and how it compares with an ontology an LLM writes or a schema mined from the data, is unknown. We make the checker itself the object of study. Across curated, LLM-generated and data-derived ontologies, used as prompt context, as a checker, or both, and across open-weight and proprietary agents on public graphs and a private graph of nearly $10^{9}$ triples, we account for what ontology-derived checks catch and what they wrongly flag. We show how each source trades caught errors against false alarms, how a curated schema used as published can contradict its own data, which errors structural checks miss, and whether combining sources or giving the agent the checker's specific feedback pays off. On UniProt, the curated ontology flags more correct answers than either an ontology derived from the data or one an LLM derives from a summary of the data with masked identifiers. On real agent answers, its flags are only marginally more precise than random flags.

Abstract: AI agents can now write software from a plain-English description, but code that runs is not the same as code that does what was asked. This gap matters most for smart contracts, the programs behind Web3 applications that move money on a blockchain and cannot be fixed once deployed. We ask how to check whether an AI-written contract behaves as it should, and where the requested behavior gets lost.
  We design an evaluation built on execution rather than opinion. We replay the same transactions on an AI-written contract and on the original it should reproduce and measure how often they agree. We test every grading tool on planted mistakes, compare against an answer key written without the description, and audit our own scorer until known wrong answers cannot pass. Common methods, such as AI judges and standard analysis tools, miss obvious mistakes, and scoring choices matter too. Checking only the functions every system includes hid about half the difference between pipelines.
  We then propose an elicitation-first architecture. Before writing code, the agent asks the client a short checklist of questions, checks the contract against an independently written executable answer key, and returns disagreements to the client instead of silently rewriting code. Asking first matters far more than reviewing or patching afterwards, across three AI models, while automated security fixes often break requested behavior. A few well-chosen questions recover much of what a short description omits, and their value can be estimated without the original contract. To our knowledge, this is the first study in smart contract synthesis to gather missing requirements before generation and check the result against an independently written executable model. The lessons extend to any setting where AI agents turn requests into programs. We release our tools, 600 tasks and all results.

</details>

Abstract: AI agents that share a secret convention can coordinate through messages that look innocent, so a monitor that reads only the messages can miss them. We propose a behavioural test: a partner that uses a code reacts to the exact wording of a message, whereas an uninvolved agent placed in its seat does not. Partner--Outsider Positive Listening (PoPLi) is the difference between the two agents in how much their action changes when the message is reworded with its meaning kept, and it is computed after the fact by replaying logged prompts. In a simulated market with five conditions (no collusion, a joint reward with neutral messages, the same with an openly stated intention, a covert first-letter code, and the code with a stated intention), PoPLi is between 0.48 and 0.59 whenever the code is present (95% intervals all above 0.29) and between -0.10 and -0.01 without it. Message-only monitors did not separate the conditions: one reading the speaker's messages answered "no" in all 50 markets, and one reading every seller's messages flagged 9 of 10 markets with no collusion. PoPLi detects the use of a private channel, not collusion as such, and it cannot detect collusion through incentives alone or codes carried by meaning.

</details>

Abstract: Language-model agents differ fundamentally from prior intelligent systems in their ability to learn from natural language in context. This capability enables agents to iteratively refine their answers in response to feedback, a prevalent mode of operation in modern agentic workflows. However, the community primarily ranks models by metrics such as Pass@1 or Pass@K, which cannot capture how they utilize feedback on prior attempts. We introduce Seq@K, a natural evaluation protocol that measures whether a model can solve a problem within K sequential attempts, provided feedback after each previous failure. Unlike multi-turn agentic benchmarks that examine long-horizon planning, Seq@K isolates and formalizes the core capability of refinement from feedback. We evaluate Seq@K on a suite of 7 benchmarks and 16 frontier models, and show that Seq@K reveals a materially different model-selection landscape: under Seq@K, (1) model rankings often differ, (2) the Pareto frontiers admit more efficient configurations and (3) the gains from choosing the optimal model configuration can widen or shrink relative to those of Pass@K. We further show that analyzing model trajectories under Seq@K helps characterize iterative refinement behavior, such as the rate at which models address issues raised in feedback, or how rigidly they hold onto their own answers. Finally, we study how the nature of feedback affects models' iterative refinement, showing that practitioners must carefully design and benchmark the feedback loops of iterative agentic systems. Together, these results establish how Seq@K offers a more faithful metric for intelligent systems that typically operate in such feedback loop, and the necessity to consider Seq@K in evaluating models for these systems.

</details>

Abstract: Food similarity underlies retrieval, recommendation, substitution, and other food computing tasks, yet food representations are typically evaluated by identity preservation. We ask what happens to food similarity beyond identity: how different visual representations organize foods into relational neighborhoods, what culinary relationships these neighborhoods reveal or obscure, and how consistently these structures emerge across representations. Indian cuisine provides a challenging setting in which visually similar foods may have different culinary identities, while foods within a culinary family can vary substantially in appearance. Using an expanded Khana dataset and seven pretrained visual representations spanning convolutional, transformer-based, self-supervised, and image-text aligned models, we evaluate image-to-image retrieval and image-to-dish neighborhoods, examining culinary-family structure, contrast cases, and cross-representation agreement. We also aggregate image representations to compare dish-level visual similarity across models and against recipe-derived similarity for the same dishes. Image-level neighborhoods vary substantially across representations, including similarities across culinary-family boundaries. At the dish level, aggregation reveals common visual structure despite local variation, but this structure corresponds less strongly with recipe-derived similarity. These findings show that recognition performance alone cannot characterize relational structure: food similarity depends on representation, modality, and abstraction level rather than being a fixed property of the food collection. Understanding these structures is therefore a prerequisite to combining multiple food information modalities into a coherent notion of similarity.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate AI assistance can improve immediate judgments while reducing opportunities to practise the operations needed for independent performance. Verification Learning Theory (VLT) formalises this possibility for claim-evaluation practice followed by correct, independent, rule-specific feedback. The model links perceived assistant reliability to evidence checking and links the history of those checks to retained rule use. Its strict form assumes that task demands and perceived reliability influence learning only through checking, with equal learning per genuine check. It therefore predicts that perceived fallibility can improve delayed unaided judgment even without observed assistant errors, and that this advantage disappears when checking is already required and performed. Rule-specific gains and forecasts from checking histories provide further tests. Simulations examine the consequences and parameter dependence of these commitments; they do not constitute empirical validation. Comparisons with depth-of-processing and feedback-encoding accounts identify where predictions diverge and where the accounts remain observationally equivalent. The framework specifies experiments that can test the proposed mechanism and separates retention of practised rules from the broader problem of recognising which rule applies to a new claim.

</details>

Abstract: Subjective judgments such as offensiveness often vary substantially across individuals, motivating pluralistic modeling approaches that incorporate annotators' socio-demographic information to capture this variation. However, prior findings on the benefits of socio-demographic information for pluralistic modeling are mixed, calling for a systematic analysis of both its effectiveness and the mechanisms through which it improves prediction. In this work, we study $\textbf{when}$ and $\textbf{where}$ socio-demographics benefit pluralistic offensiveness modeling, as well as $\textbf{the role of explicit reasoning}$ in leveraging this information. Across three multi-annotator datasets with prompted and trained LLMs, we first show that demographic gains are small and inconsistent for individual predictions but become clearer at the group and population levels, because aggregation attenuates the idiosyncratic variation that masks small but systematic demographic signals. Second, using a reasoning and prediction format that explicitly decomposes individual judgment into an overall baseline and a demographic-specific deviation, we show that the gains arise from both components: demographic information improves baseline estimation and enables meaningful demographic-specific shifts, and interventions show that better component estimates improve final predictions. Finally, comparing direct demographic conditioning with explicit reasoning about how the text interacts with annotator demographics, we find that reasoning yields more accurate baseline and shift estimates as well as final predictions, whereas conditioning alone rarely produces informative shifts. These results suggest that the mixed evidence in prior work may partly stem from individual-level evaluation, and that the value of socio-demographic information depends on both how it is evaluated and how models are guided to use it.

</details>

Abstract: Public disclosure repositories such as SEC EDGAR support informed investment through web access to corporate filings. Supporting their review with language model agents requires discovering unstated deficiencies across related documents, acquiring missing company facts, and verifying that requested revisions satisfy evidence-backed requirements. Existing financial question-answering evaluations provide limited evidence of these interactive capabilities. We introduce \textbf{FilingLoop}, an interactive benchmark and a self-evolving agent framework for corporate disclosure review and revision. Grounded in historical U.S.\ Securities and Exchange Commission correspondence, FilingLoop reconstructs 380 predominantly IPO review tasks from 96 companies under distinct information constraints. An evaluator separate from the acting agents scores final filings and responses against 6,099 eligible requirements and audits unsupported claims and harmful revisions. Our framework couples iterative review and revision within each case with procedural learning across cases, converting requirement-level evaluation feedback into role-specific guidance without updating model weights. Updates affect only subsequent cases, allowing procedural adaptation to be assessed without retrying scored tasks. On the evaluated stream, evolution increases requirement fulfillment from 24.28\% to 27.96\% and full comment resolution from 16.90\% to 20.75\%, with a 5.8\% increase in total cost over the fixed-policy variant. Abstract: Spiking reservoir computing combines the training efficiency of fixed recurrent reservoirs with the energy efficiency of event-driven inference, where computation is triggered by sparse spikes rather than executed densely at every timestep. However, existing spiking reservoirs almost exclusively rely on Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) neurons, whose low-pass dynamics limit their ability to distinguish information encoded at different temporal frequencies. We introduce HRF-Res, a liquid state machine in which LIF neurons are replaced by a heterogeneous population of Harmonic Resonate-and-Fire (HRF) neurons. These neurons exhibit intrinsic oscillatory dynamics and selectively respond to input frequencies, enabling the reservoir to capture frequency-specific temporal patterns. The result is more discriminative representations and higher classification accuracy than LIF-based reservoirs, achieved without sacrificing the energy efficiency of spiking computation, with up to 19x lower energy consumption than an equivalent non-spiking oscillatory reservoir. Evaluated on ten benchmarks spanning time series, sequential images, audio spike trains, and neuromorphic vision, HRF-Res achieves state-of-the-art accuracy among spiking reservoir methods. These results demonstrate that introducing frequency-selective dynamics into spiking reservoirs improves representational quality and classification performance while preserving their inherent energy efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model agents are increasingly used for scientific discovery. However, existing benchmarks mix law inference with programming, score data fitting instead of prediction on unseen inputs, and do not separate empirical evidence from pretrained priors. We introduce SciGame, a controlled benchmark that converts scientific literature into structured scientific cards. Each card yields a pair of Original and dimensionally consistent Counterfactual environments with the same task interface. SciGame contains a Historical collection of canonical laws and a live Temporal collection curated from papers published after model knowledge cutoffs. It evaluates agents in candidate-guided Identification and open-ended Discovery settings. Each setting runs under Prior-only, Fixed, and Adaptive protocols. Experiments on five frontier models yield three findings. First, familiar priors hurt performance once the governing relation is altered. Second, adaptive experimentation helps the strongest models but harms weaker ones under limited budgets. Third, good explanations can hide quantitative failures. Recovering the exact symbolic form remains the main bottleneck, even when agents interpret evidence correctly. Current agents can investigate systems actively, but inducing exact laws in unfamiliar settings remains an open challenge.

Abstract: Across human history, knowledge has undergone several transitions, from orality to writing and then to digital archives. At each transition, humans established new conventions to preserve knowledge for the new era. Nowadays, as research agents are increasingly deployed for laboratory experiments, knowledge is crossing another boundary: from records written for humans to those for machines. In this work, we introduce VerifiedLab, a multi-agent harness framework for this human-to-machine transition of knowledge. VerifiedLab transforms research output from passive artifacts into active systems that accelerate downstream use, adoption and prediction of future trends. In VerifiedLab, a team of AI scientists with distinct roles reasons over records verified field by field: a principal investigator plans, a librarian computes the evidence directly from the records, specialists draft, and a gate and a critic check each claim against the measured reliability of its data before the harness decides whether to release or withhold it. We demonstrate the efficacy of VerifiedLab through extensive experiments on perovskite solar cells: VerifiedLab recovers known physical trends, anticipates devices reported a year later, and lets AI scientists inherit the literature together with a measure of how far it can be trusted. By scaling the inference-time verification compute, VerifiedLab introduces a new pathway for knowledge dissemination and machine harness for AI co-scientists.

</details>

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) enables teams of agents, such as robots and autonomous vehicles, to learn coordinated behavior, but what such teams learn is largely determined by their reward function. Because task success alone provides too little guidance for learning, intermediate rewards are usually designed by hand, and this effort must often be repeated whenever the environment or the team changes. Large language models (LLMs) can automate the effort by writing reward functions as code, yet the resulting rewards are typically designed and tested in a single setting. To enable their reuse across settings, GARDEN (Generalist Autonomous Reward Design through Evidence Navigation) is proposed, in which one reward program is developed for a family of related scenarios and transferred, unchanged, to scenarios that were not available during its design. During development, environment code, training records, and agent behavior are investigated by an LLM reward engineer, and draft rewards are tested in trial training runs, while the learning algorithm and the rule for selecting the final reward are held fixed. On unseen scenarios of the Overcooked and SMAX benchmarks, task returns were increased by 58.2% and 52.5% over the default rewards on two kitchen layouts, and the mean win rate in two combat scenarios was raised from 51.0% to 89.9%. Under matched candidate and probe budgets, this exceeded the 84.2% and 79.2% obtained with fixed diagnostic feedback and Eureka-inspired reward reflection. These results show that a single autonomously engineered reward can be reused effectively across related cooperative scenarios.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as potential tools for numerical optimization. Despite promising results, their intrinsic optimization capabilities and limitations remain poorly understood, particularly in high dimensions. This paper investigates the ability of LLMs to exploit mathematical problem structures and generate feasible, near-optimal solutions. We develop an evaluation framework that distinguishes solution quality from execution failures, including numerical inaccuracies, constraint violations, and output-format errors. Using representative problems from convex optimization, fractional programming, and constrained projection, we assess LLM performance against deterministic reference solvers across different dimensions. Our results reveal that LLMs can capture useful optimization structures and produce near-optimal solutions, even when their outputs fail to satisfy numerical or formatting requirements. However, reliability deteriorates with increasing problem dimension and depends strongly on the underlying optimization structure. To further investigate the limitations, we deploy an iterative refinement mechanism that provides targeted feedback on feasibility and solution quality. The results demonstrate that iterative refinement substantially improves reliability and extends the range of problem dimensions over which LLMs produce valid solutions. Nevertheless, persistent numerical and structural errors limit their applicability as standalone optimizers. These findings provide insights into the capacity and limits of LLM-based optimization and motivate hybrid approaches that combine LLM reasoning with deterministic numerical verification.

</details>

Abstract: Latent multi-agent systems reduce inference cost by keeping intermediate reasoning and inter-agent communication in continuous representations.\ Reusable skills, however, are commonly supplied as text or stored as layer-wise Key-Value states or skill-specific model parameters, introducing repeated processing or substantial per-skill storage.\ We introduce \textbf{SkillMem}, which represents each reusable skill as a small external latent memory and makes it directly available during latent multi-agent reasoning.\ A role-conditioned cross-attention reader allows the same memory to guide different agents according to their roles and current hidden states.\ We train the skill memories and shared reader through behavioral distillation, matching the change in token predictions induced by a textual skill while keeping the backbone frozen.\ For our experiments, we obtain textual skills from paired successful and failed multi-agent trajectories, although existing skills can also be compiled directly.\ Across six benchmarks and Qwen3 models ranging from 4B to 14B parameters, SkillMem provides strong gains for smaller backbones while preserving efficient latent multi-agent inference across model scales.\ We use the same $8\times256$ memory configuration across all evaluated model scales, requiring 9.6\,KB of skill-specific storage per skill, including its retrieval embedding.\ For a library of 100 skills, SkillMem reduces total storage by more than $38\times$ compared with alternative skill representations and achieves up to a $4.23\times$ end-to-end inference speedup.\ These results show that compact procedural memories can be reused without sacrificing the efficiency of latent multi-agent reasoning.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) inference has become an operational workload that fleets must provision, monitor, and bill against latency service-level objectives (SLOs). A single throughput number cannot answer the questions operators actually face, because generative serving couples two phases with very different service characteristics, continuous batching reshapes the effective service process, and user-visible tail latency can degrade long before aggregate throughput saturates. We present GAUSS, a workload-aware analytical and Monte-Carlo simulator that predicts the full distributions of time-to-first-token (TTFT) and time-between-tokens (TBT), together with the sustainable request rate, for a given workload, engine, and accelerator. GAUSS pairs calibrated, phase-specific latency surfaces for prefill and decode with a Lindley-recurrence model of queueing under stochastic arrivals and estimates performance by ensemble Monte-Carlo sampling rather than by replaying a single trace. This lets GAUSS reason about workloads, routing policies, and hardware that have never been deployed, and to capture the latency tails that trace-driven simulators systematically under-sample. We describe the model and show how it supports operating-point selection, traffic segmentation, and shortest-remaining-processing-time scheduling capacity questions that trace-driven simulators cannot address.

</details>

Abstract: Advancing language models for specialized scientific applications requires evaluations of scientific fluency, physical reasoning, and quantitative analyses. Using inertial confinement fusion (ICF) as a test case, we have developed an expert-curated benchmark of 250 questions spanning qualitative reasoning, numerical calculation, symbolic derivation, and equation formulation. Responses have been evaluated using deterministic numerical and symbolic checks together with language-model judges. We compared a variety of out-of-the-box closed-source and open-source large language models with Prospero, a model specialized through combined domain-adaptive pretraining and supervised fine-tuning for ICF. We also conducted initial retrieval-assisted evaluations. Prospero improved its aggregate score by 10.7 percentage points over its base model, with gains across all question types. Frontier models achieved the highest aggregate scores, but critical physics errors persisted, particularly in numerical calculations. We note that domain-specific performance can improve through specialization, but benchmark gains alone do not establish scientific reliability. Retrieval improved performance for a smaller model but yielded no statistically significant improvement for a frontier model. Two scientific stress tests further illustrate useful capabilities and the need for expert assessment of physical assumptions and model validity. Based on these findings, we recommend evaluating existing domain capabilities and retrieval benefits before committing to substantial additional specialization.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) appear to represent and reason about a large range of phenomena. In particular, recent studies have examined whether LLMs can accurately reason about causation. One of the most commonly used benchmarks for evaluating formal causal reasoning in LLMs is CLadder, a framework for procedural generation of benchmark data sets that was released in 2023. Experiments with CLadder appear to indicate that LLMs can perform formal causal reasoning in a large percentage of generated examples. In this paper, we examine what can be reliably inferred when LLMs perform well on test sets produced by CLadder. We identify several examples of superficial and highly predictive patterns that the CLadder generator embeds in data sets it generates. We find that even simple models such as random forests perform well on CLadder-generated data sets after training on a sample of generated data. We also find that the performance of many commercial LLMs on CLadder test sets is extremely brittle and that removing large sections of the text that are critical for formal causal reasoning has almost no effect on performance. Given these results, the most likely hypothesis for the strong performance of state-of-the-art LLMs on CLadder is that they are exploiting superficial patterns learned from exposure to training data from the generator. Any inference that LLMs are exhibiting more complex causal reasoning must rule out this hypothesis.

</details>
