Abstract: Software vulnerability detection remains difficult because existing approaches rely on predefined analysis rules, learn vulnerability patterns implicitly from code and labels, or use large language models (LLMs) to make the final security judgment. It remains unclear whether structured descriptions of security-relevant program behavior can improve supervised detection.
  We present VulnLens, an explanation-augmented framework for binary C/C++ vulnerability detection that separates program-behavior analysis from the final decision. A label-blind LLM-based Explainer examines each function from seven fixed perspectives: data flow, memory access, object lifetime, arithmetic and type behavior, control flow, shared state, and external-resource access. Without receiving vulnerability labels or CWE information, it generates structured explanations. A supervised Detector combines source code and explanations through sequential cross-attention.
  We evaluate VulnLens on a PrimeVul-derived dataset covering ten CWE categories and test transfer to SecVulEval without retraining. On the 526-function PrimeVul test set, VulnLens achieves 75.5% accuracy and 77.2% F1, exceeding DeepSeek V4 Pro by 4.2 percentage points in accuracy and Gemini 2.5 Pro by 2.2 points in F1. Matched explanations improve F1 by 7.3 points over a separately trained code-only detector. On SecVulEval, VulnLens achieves 66.4% accuracy and 65.8% F1, outperforming DeepSeek V4 Pro by 7.6 points in accuracy and 20.0 points in F1, and Gemini 2.5 Pro by 16.6 points in accuracy and 7.8 points in F1.

Abstract: AI compiler and runtime stacks must preserve bindings between tensor values and their operations, storage, and execution state. These bindings can fail while the underlying objects remain present, with symptoms appearing in another layer. We present TensorBound, an empirical study of tensor binding failures in IREE and the XLA and PyTorch ecosystems. The study analyzes 264 reports and reconstructs 149 distinct repaired causes through source tracing and comparisons of pre-fix and repaired revisions. Each reconstruction connects the first invalid decision to its violated condition, physical effect, and repair. Missing eligibility checks, incorrect mappings or translations, and lost or omitted relations account for 69.1% of repaired causes. These patterns highlight the need to preserve binding facts and check the conditions for their use. The 101 first invalid decisions in compiler code identify candidate checking points before execution. Four archived IREE diagnostic attempts distinguish effect localization from source-based cause explanation. Together, these findings motivate transition-specific checks that preserve binding facts across representations and enforce the required conditions at consuming decisions.

Abstract: Public exploit code for a newly disclosed vulnerability usually appears before any published rule that detects its exploitation. Of the 45 CVEs we evaluate, 37 ever received a Sigma, Elastic or Splunk rule; in 33 the exploit came first, by a median of 22 days. Analysts still write every rule by hand. We present Encore, which compiles a public Python proof-of-concept exploit (PoC) into CVE-specific hunting rules over a web service's existing logs, without running it. Encore rests on the PoC Behavior Alignment Principle: a PoC performs the exploit, so its code already provides a rule's HTTP requests, payloads and host operations. That is hard to act on: a PoC is written against libraries and runs on the attacker's machine, while the target's web-server, proxy and host logs record only what arrived and what it did. Encore closes the gap with a map from library calls to the operations they cause, built once per library, keeping only the operations visible on the target. For 12 of the 45, Encore's rule states the exploit's own payload fragment or host operation, which is evidence of the exploit itself, and not of traffic to its endpoint. On matched controls it separates that exploit from the same request with a harmless value, and it raises no alerts across 27,081 legitimate and crawl requests. We never compile the exploit that produced the logs. Most are Splunk's public attack data, recorded by a third party. Without reading a single log, Encore's rules find 43 of the 45. Splunk's log-tested rules cover 30 of the 45, and Encore finds 28 of those, plus all 10 CVEs without an ESCU rule. Where Splunk's rules alert on 20 wrong CVE pairs, Encore's alert on only 2. Hunting can start as soon as a PoC is public, and rules can raise an alert when it provides a payload or host operation.

</details>

On a 556-case leaderboard subset of SkillTrustBench, it achieved 95.90% recall with a 4.22% false positive rate; on a balanced sample of 1,000 MalSkillBench skills, its F1 was 0.947, outperforming Cisco's Skill Scanner, NVIDIA's SkillSpector, AgentSkillsScanner, and SkillSieve. In 1,587 skills from the 75 most-starred GitHub repositories, three analysts confirmed 53 of 59 findings (89.8% precision) across 23 repositories. We developed proof-of-concept (PoC) attacks for each vulnerability, disclosed 10 medium- and high-severity cases to maintainers, and tested mitigations on the remaining cases. Composite Analysis models dependencies among skills through shared resources, invocations, and instruction-mediated interactions. On SCR-Bench, its F1 score was 0.991 (98.7% precision, 99.6% recall), versus 0.816-0.858 for three SkillsGuard configurations released with CompoSkill. It identified two composite findings on GitHub, validated by PoC attacks. The Mitigation module recommends skill substitution, patches, and configuration changes. For 47 of 55 GitHub findings, mitigations prevented PoC attacks in all trials while preserving tested legitimate functionality.

Abstract: AI-generated non-consensual intimate imagery (AIG-NCII) is an increasingly prevalent form of online abuse, distinguished from AI-generated sexual content (AIG-SC) by the sexualized depiction of a real person's identity without their consent. To understand how offenders may benefit from the tools and resources shared across the broader AIG-SC community, we analyzed 139 R+ guides on CivitAI, a popular platform known to host a disproportionate amount of AIG-SC.
  We find that AIG-SC guides discuss how to create content generation workflows composed of sexual and identity concepts, the building blocks of AIG-NCII, including one guide that explicitly outlines an AIG-NCII workflow. We identify that ultimately AIG-NCII is enabled by configuration rather than a distinct set of technical tools. We map AIG-NCII threat models across basic and advanced AIG-SC workflows to demonstrate how multiple dual-use technical components can compose NCII outputs in an environment with little to no discussion of ethical implications or responsible use. We conclude by noting that, as most workflows rely on open-weight models, existing technical defenses against AIG-NCII are limited. However, our findings offer a starting point for evaluation of AI-based models and products against realistic attacker behavior required by new EU regulation and suggest that efforts to develop safeguards at the model- and product-levels should focus on generation of identity content.

We also separate logical-source from transport commitments so lossless re-encoding need not invalidate unchanged scientific evidence. The SCENE prototype evaluates signed revisions, authenticated queries, scoped disclosures, and adversarial mutations at scales reaching one million source records. These guarantees rely on declared policies and independently pinned trust roots; they authenticate specified computations and obligations, not historical truth, complete counterevidence, or institutional endorsement.

Abstract: Physical-layer fingerprinting (PLF) has emerged as a promising authentication approach for Internet of Things devices. However, most PLF systems acquire and process a fixed-length waveform for every authentication query, burdening resource-constrained edge nodes. To address this issue, we propose a progressive-observation PLF method that adapts the waveform length to each query. Specifically, a lightweight convolutional neural network (CNN) first rejects clearly unknown devices from a short observation. Spectral matching then accepts clearly registered devices from a slightly longer observation. Only ambiguous queries proceed to the same matching on the full-length waveform, reducing both data acquisition and computation. Because fingerprints drift across days, we also design an adaptive calibration algorithm to avoid retraining the CNN. The algorithm realigns the features and decision thresholds using a few current-day references from registered devices. We evaluate the method with 100BASE-TX Ethernet devices of two controller types on held-out days. Compared with fixed-length processing, it keeps the false rejection and false acceptance rates equal or lower for both controller types on both days. It also reduces the waveform data per query by 75.1% and the CPU processing time on a Raspberry Pi 5 by 73.7% on average. With the same monitoring bandwidth, an edge node can therefore serve about four times as many authentication queries.

</details>

Abstract: Deep neural networks (DNNs) are widely deployed in safety- and privacy-critical domains such as healthcare and finance, where models are trained on confidential data. This paper presents the first hardware-level attribute-stealing attack that bypasses state-of-the-art privacy defenses, revealing that accelerator optimizations intended for performance can directly undermine data confidentiality. Instead, we show that sparsity-driven execution paths encode stable timing signatures that act as a surrogate for model confidence, enabling high-fidelity attribute inference from a single query. SparseBleed generalizes across heterogeneous backends and hardware engines, including Intel AVX2/AVX-512/AMX, NVIDIA Ampere Tensor Cores with 2:4 sparsity, and nine well-known sparse and dense libraries such as Intel oneDNN, oneMKL SparseBLAS, and NVIDIA cuSPARSE. We evaluate SparseBleed to infer sensitive input attributes and true output labels. On Texas-100X, SparseBleed infers sensitive input attributes such as Sex with 66.39% accuracy and recovers the true output label, Surgical Procedure, with 99.8% accuracy. Finally, we evaluate mitigations. Constant-time execution by disabling sparsity optimizations is expensive (e.g., up to $2\times$ higher latency on NVIDIA Sparse Tensor Cores and $\approx 25\%$ higher power in AMX microbenchmarks). By contrast, a padding defense that equalizes responses to the worst-case time masks timing leakage with only 7.24% average performance overhead across nine libraries on Texas-100X/Adult and near-zero extra energy.

Abstract: Web assistants increasingly answer questions about a user's private documents by pairing a small on-device model with a cloud LLM behind a Web API. In collaborative decoding, the document stays on the client, and the cloud receives only a public view that the user approves, such as the question alone, but the two models exchange messages while the answer is generated. We show that the message transcript can disclose private content that neither the public view nor the answer contains. In a planted-secret audit, speculative decoding with private drafts did not include the secret code in its answers, yet sent the code to the cloud in rejected drafts. We present ADLER, a decoding protocol for streaming LLM APIs in which every message to the cloud depends only on the public view and the answer the user sees, regardless of how the client combines the two models or how the cloud behaves. A client-side leakage ledger then bounds how much the answer itself reveals relative to a public reference process built from the cloud's replies. In the audit, no ADLER configuration exposed the secret beyond the answer. On GSM8K math problems with numerical values masked in the cloud's input, ADLER with an unlimited leakage budget raises the exact-match accuracy of a 0.5B client from 0.41 to 0.83 and of a 1.5B client from 0.67 to 0.85 under lenient answer parsing, close to the 0.92 achieved by uploading the full problem. It also exceeds a zero-leakage program baseline (0.69) by 0.14 and 0.16, respectively. In this baseline, the cloud writes a program that the client runs offline using the private numbers. The ledger records the disclosure charge associated with this gain; budgets of 16 nats or less leave accuracy near the cloud-only baseline on GSM8K.

Abstract: Modern enterprises increasingly operate through complex information systems (IS) enabled by cloud computing, remote and hybrid work practices, and digitally integrated supply chains. These structural shifts have expanded organizational attack surfaces and weakened traditional perimeter-based trust assumptions, giving rise to new and persistent forms of cyber threats. In response, adversaries have shifted toward identity-centric, trust-based intrusion techniques that exploit legitimate access within organizational systems. This evolution has created a pronounced speed asymmetry, whereby attackers can adapt and act at machine speed while defenders remain constrained by reactive or rule-based incident response (IR) processes. Drawing on design science research, we propose ZT-AIR, an adaptive IR artifact that uses zero trust architecture as its design theory and hierarchical reinforcement learning as its kernel theory. ZT-AIR learns context-aware trust decisions, separates strategic prioritization from tactical execution, encodes zero-trust controls as actions, and jointly optimizes security effectiveness and operational continuity. Experimental results across diverse adversarial strategies, network scales, and defense environments show that ZT-AIR achieves a strong balance between security and continuity through frequent, minimally disruptive interventions. Practitioner validation further confirms the design's practical viability. This study contributes to IS research by extending computational design science from threat identification to response execution, theorizing zero trust architecture for adaptive IR, and deriving reusable design principles for adaptive decision support artifacts.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based agents now share experiences on social platforms and remember new preferences to enhance their performance. However, existing memory systems contain a vulnerability in role-to-principal attribution that attackers can exploit. We identify Role Grounding Misattribution, in which an attacker-designed interaction causes the victim agent to incorrectly record a preference from the attacker as a preference of the victim's user. Specifically, we are the first to formalize Role Grounding Attack (RGA), an attack model that exploits this vulnerability through nested conversation structures. In the memory formation stage, an experience-sharing post quoting a conversation between the attacker agent and its user is published, which explicitly identifies its publisher. The inner conversation expresses preferences from the user of the attacker agent. When the victim agent remembers preferences from this nested structure, its memory loses the context binding and grounds the attacker's preference to the victim's user instead. In the memory retrieval stage, the misattributed preference persists and influences the victim agent's decisions when it encounters relevant scenarios. We instantiate RGA as tool selection manipulation and privacy leakage. Tool selection manipulation causes a tool-call preference to be misattributed to the victim's user, steering the victim toward an attacker-controlled tool. Privacy leakage extends this by misattributing both a tool-call preference and a task-requirement preference to the victim's user. The victim selects the attacker's tool, then includes sensitive information in tool arguments to satisfy the task requirement. We evaluate RGA across five mainstream memory systems and six language models. Tool manipulation and privacy leakage both achieve high attack success rates. These results call for stronger role grounding.

</details>

Abstract: Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) enables computation on encrypted data and is promising for privacy-preserving computing. However, FHE deployment remains constrained due to massive computational, memory, and data-movement overheads. Existing acceleration strategies often overlook the storage I/O bottleneck caused by the enormous size of FHE data in large-scale applications, as most prior works focus on small benchmarks that fit in main memory. Our studies show that state-of-the-art FHE systems spend 71\% to 85\% of their execution time moving data between storage and compute units rather than performing useful computation. To address the I/O bottleneck, we present Rosemary, a heterogeneous FHE acceleration system that incorporates a novel in-storage processing (ISP) architecture with compiler-level and algorithmic optimizations. Our key innovations include 1) an adaptable hardware-aware FHE engine inside a NAND flash-based solid-state drive (SSD) for efficient and cost-effective in-storage FHE processing, 2) a Throughput-Data-Aware Program Allocator (TDAP) that partitions FHE workloads into heterogeneous programs, and 3) a Compiler-Level Ciphertext-Aware Optimizer (CLCAO) that exploits ISP-friendly FHE kernels for low-cost ISP execution. Across a diverse set of FHE workloads, including encrypted deep learning inference and cloud database queries, Rosemary achieves geometric-mean speedups of $185.7\times$ over state-of-the-art CPU/GPU implementations and $3.7\times$ over prior FHE accelerators, while improving energy efficiency by up to $3.9\times$ and Energy-Delay-Area-Product (EDAP) by up to $16.5\times$.

</details>

Abstract: As the advent of large-scale quantum computing threatens classical encryption, Quasi-Cyclic Moderate Density Parity-Check (QC-MDPC) codes have emerged as a foundational element of Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC). However, decoding these relatively dense codes using classical Belief Propagation algorithms often leads to failures caused by short graph cycles that stall the decoder. In cryptographic applications, these failures are severe security vulnerabilities that leak secret key information. To address this, this paper proposes a Recurrent Bipartite Graph Neural Network (R-BGNN) as proof of concept. By integrating Gated Recurrent Units (GRUs) into the message-passing framework and employing a multi-topology curriculum training strategy, the R-BGNN actively learns to break limit-cycle oscillations. Evaluated across 20 unseen matrix topologies, the proposed architecture significantly outperforms classical Bit-Flipping and Min-Sum baselines, reducing the Decoding Failure Rate (DFR) by 63.7\% at moderate noise levels ($t=10$) while converging in fewer iterations.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have enabled more effective vulnerability scanning of web applications. WebMaze embeds functionality-coupled traps in genuine pages and constructs view-tailored interactive decoy worlds from the application's existing views. The traps blend into genuine components and expose entry points to decoy worlds that sustain exploration through connected pages and interactions without changing genuine functionality. Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) traffic-signal controllers may use vehicle-to-infrastructure (V2I) reports, exposing their perception layer to false-data injection. Existing demonstrations typically assume either white-box access to the controller's weights or full observability of the traffic state. We study the impact of a weaker and more realistic adversary who has no access to the RL model and whose traffic observations are limited to her own approach. This black-box, minimally informed attacker trains an imitation surrogate of the controller. She then crafts bounded fake reports, restricted to inflating vehicle counts on her own approach, that transfer to the real controller. We demonstrate that despite the loss of accuracy relative to an attacker who can observe the entire state and action space, this limited attacker can significantly increase the average waiting time for other approaches while barely changing the waiting time on her own approach, i.e. externalizing the added delay onto the other approaches. Because the only realizable move is to inflate one's own reported queue, we find that a zero-knowledge attacker that simply reports the maximum feasible queue is as damaging as the surrogate-based one, while an unstructured random inflation is not: the surrogate is not required to reproduce the degradation, and the exposed vulnerability is the V2I sensing channel itself. The surrogate's role is directional control, selecting a disruptive or a selfish objective at the same budget. The attack persists under +/-20% uniform demand variation. Finally, on a common per-lane budget we place the attacker on a knowledge ladder against white-box adversaries, and find the black-box surrogate matches a realizable white-box attacker that uses the victim's own gradients; only abandoning realizability raises the damage further.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly acting through Web interfaces and external tools. They produce trajectories. Those trajectories may pass through several intermediaries. They can store, modify, or dispute those trajectories. In Web-based agent deployments, trajectories may pass through APIs, marketplaces, and tool servers. As such, a copied or disputed log may not by itself reveal which agent owner produced it. Behavioral watermarking can embed ownership evidence in agent action choices. However, existing methods mainly address detection, removal, rewriting, or imitation. They do not directly test whether repeated observation of watermarked trajectories enables cross-session forgery by exploiting learned action preferences. We formulate this learned-forgery threat and propose AgentAnchor, a session-keyed behavioral watermark for owner attribution from trajectory logs. We derive each step key from a secret key, a fresh public session identifier, and semantic states of recent observations, while a guarded digest of the action-policy state diversifies linear coding rows. It embeds parity evidence through a KL-bounded exponential tilt, and a trusted verifier checks a disputed log without rerunning the agent, using wrong-key calibration and abstention.

Abstract: Protecting Internet services from attacks such as TLS/HTTP floods requires rate limiting, yet identifying users to enforce these limits can compromise their privacy. Anonymous rate limiting promises to reconcile these goals, but existing schemes either struggle to scale or assume that services and authorities do not collude. We present STAR, which uses server-side trusted execution environments (TEEs) to overcome these limitations. By enabling lightweight cryptographic operations, TEEs make authentication efficient, but introduce a privacy challenge: malicious services can observe memory-access patterns to infer user secrets. We address this threat through countermeasures against secret-dependent memory accesses and assess our implementation using dynamic analysis. To extend these efficiency gains across servers, we also design a secure sharding mechanism that distributes client requests. We demonstrate that strong privacy guarantees can coexist with efficient authentication at scale; STAR processes authentication requests in tens of microseconds and handles approximately 13.9K requests per second per CPU core.

</details>

Abstract: Cryptographically verifiable C2PA-based Content Credentials (CCs) can be attached to freshly acquired or generated digital images but also to already existing ones with no previous provenance information, for instance after processing through trusted C2PA-enabled software tools. The latter case leads to images with so-called partial CCs, which capture only the last operations applied, while prior provenance remains unknown and can be investigated through signal forensics. This paper addresses the forensic analysis of such images, highlighting the opportunity to leverage the partial CCs as additional trusted knowledge to adapt forensic detectors towards the statistical characteristics of the image under analysis and mitigate potential distribution shifts. To this end, a two-step strategy is envisioned: (i) reconstructing the operations applied by the last signer through a structural analysis of the partial CCs; (ii) exploiting the obtained reconstruction to enhance the forensic detector of the analyst through an adaptation process. An empirical analysis on partial CCs produced by commercial C2PA-enabled software tools through different editing operations is carried out, providing evidence on the feasibility of the reconstruction in practical cases. An experimental case study on the forensic task of synthetic image detection is then presented, showing that the proposed strategy can significantly enhance the performance of state-of-the-art detectors on test images that are postprocessed via C2PA-enabled software tools after being generated/acquired.

Abstract: Differential privacy (DP) has become the standard for privacy-preserving data analysis, yet its practical implementation in federated or distributed settings often faces a significant utility gap compared to the central model. While existing frameworks, including local, shuffle, and secure aggregation models, offer vetted primitives, they can incur high error for important tasks such as selection. Our first contribution is to show that this is inherent in the aggregation model: any private selection algorithm relying on the standard noisy aggregation primitive requires $Ω(\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon)$ samples. To address this limitation, we propose adding a new primitive that implements a version of the Sparse Vector Technique (SVT) and argue that it has simple implementations under various trust models. We demonstrate its power by developing an $\varepsilon$-differentially private algorithm for the selection problem. For selection in dimension $d$, our algorithm achieves an additive error of $O\left(\frac{\log(dn)}{\varepsilon}\right)$, matching that of the central model when $n$ is polynomially bounded in $d$. Furthermore, we show that this can be done using a near-optimal expected number $O(d\log^*d)$ of SVT queries. Together with existing lower bounds for the shuffle model, this result establishes an exponential separation between the SVT model and shuffle or aggregation-based models. We demonstrate the model's broad applicability to diverse tasks such as $F_p$ loss minimization, heavy-hitter identification, and sparse mean estimation.

Abstract: Coding agents increasingly develop, test, and integrate software whose components are owned by different developers, teams, or services. This exposes a distinct attack surface: a malicious or compromised peer development role can manipulate a live component of the software under development (e.g., a backend service, test harness, CI job, or deployment service) and influence the runtime feedback that another role's coding agent must process to complete its task. Although such a component has legitimate diagnostic authority to report its own state, it has no action authority over the victim's local files, credentials, shell, or development environment. We introduce Cross-Boundary Adversarial Runtime Injection (CARI), a direct runtime-feedback injection setting that exploits this authority mismatch. Rather than explicitly compromising the victim's prompt, model, tools, or local environment, the attacker covertly embeds malicious instructions in task-relevant runtime feedback produced by a peer-controlled development component. The victim then interprets that feedback as part of normal debugging or recovery, and may translate the diagnostic guidance into unauthorized victim-side actions. We instantiate ten different attacks based on CARI, spanning resource exhaustion, information exposure, and development-state manipulation. Across four programming languages and five code-capable models, CARI succeeds in 2,845/3,000 (94.8%) trials. Furthermore, CARI transfers effectively to product-level coding agents, achieving 61.3% attack success on Codex and 58.7% on Claude Code. By contrast, prompt-level defenses exhibit a security-utility trade-off: generic warnings are ineffective, whereas explicit policies suppress attacks but substantially over-refuse legitimate actions.

</details>

Abstract: Anomaly-based network intrusion detection systems (NIDS) can detect previously unseen attacks by identifying deviations from benign traffic. However, deep learning-based anomaly detectors typically rely on implicit decision boundaries, and post-hoc explanations may not faithfully reflect the detection logic. This makes alerts difficult to interpret and audit. To address this issue, we present IDSForge, an anomaly-based NIDS that directly learns explicit, executable behavioral rules from benign traffic, making its detection logic interpretable by design. IDSForge uses the same rule set for detection and explanation, ensuring explanation fidelity by construction. It uses an explicit distributional autoencoder trained only on benign samples to learn axis-aligned regions of benign traffic. A high-speed rule compiler then consolidates these instance-specific regions into a compact set of feature-level interval constraints. These rules can be executed directly by popular NIDS engines, supporting high-throughput inspection with low overhead. To adapt to changes in traffic, a self-distillation co-evolver updates the model and rule set incrementally when the false-positive rate on analyst-verified traffic exceeds a target threshold. Our evaluation shows that IDSForge reduces F1 error by $5.7\times$ to $47\times$ relative to state-of-the-art anomaly-based baselines. Deployed in Suricata, IDSForge sustains a throughput of 7.8 Gbps with a median per-packet latency overhead of 3.1 $μ\text{s}$.

Abstract: Typed decision models are an emerging class of language models that make decisions instead of generating free-text responses. They answer in a single fast and cheap query and are increasingly used, including in security-sensitive tasks. However, red teaming of these models leaves two gaps: existing adaptive attacks do not use their speed, and few studies have searched for universal adversarial examples. We fill both gaps on Jev, the representative typed decision model, with a quick black-box query-based attack that calls no attacker language model. We conduct extensive experiments on 12 datasets with different data types and application scenarios. The results show that Jev resists common prompt injections: no strategy changes a correct answer in 10,240 paired tests. In contrast, our attack finds short suffixes that flip confident answers within minutes. These suffixes are universal: one suffix per dataset flips 43% of unseen documents. Because typed decision models, unlike classic NLP models, can serve many tasks, we test whether these suffixes transfer across tasks. We find that one suffix generated from one task can flip 90-100% of the samples in other tasks. We therefore believe the evaluation of typed decision models must include strict adaptive attacks to fully reveal their robustness.

Abstract: Optimal power flow (OPF) is a foundational analysis in power system operation, and inaccuracies in OPF decision-making can result in significant operational and security consequences. With the increasing adoption of machine-learning-based OPF surrogate models for real-time and large-scale applications, power system operation has become more susceptible to cyber threats, including data manipulation and false data injection attacks. Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI), particularly Shapley value-based approaches, has been employed to quantify the influence of input features on OPF outputs. Features exhibiting high influence are commonly interpreted as vulnerable. However, this interpretation neglects the role of attack detection mechanisms in the system, which typically monitor input-to-input relationships to detect anomalous behavior. Consequently, highly influential features may be readily detectable and may not represent the most vulnerable attack surfaces. To address this limitation, we propose a defense-aware explainable AI framework that explicitly incorporates attack detectability alongside operational impact. The framework integrates an OPF surrogate model representing input-to-output dependencies with a vanilla autoencoder representing input-to-input dependencies. Shapley-based explainability is applied to both models to construct a feature-level vulnerability metric. Empirical evaluation on IEEE test systems demonstrates that the proposed framework more accurately identifies security-relevant vulnerabilities than conventional OPF-surrogate-model-only explainability approaches.

</details>

Abstract: Automatically validating AI-generated security patches remains a significant challenge. Eliminating a reported crash and passing existing test suites do not provide sufficient confidence that a patch correctly addresses the underlying vulnerability without introducing regressions. We present DeepDiff, a technique for regression-freedom checking that differentially evaluates candidate patches against the original vulnerable program using non-crashing inputs that exercise the affected code paths. The expectation of regression-freedom is that a correct patch should eliminate the vulnerability triggering crash while preserving the intended behavior on non-crashing inputs. To support this evaluation, we introduce a dataset of correct and incorrect patch candidates for open-source memory-safety vulnerabilities from CyberGym. We explore different approaches for non-triggering test input generation to reach the patched code, a purely neural regression-freedom checker, and a hybrid approach that combines dynamic analysis with neural techniques. Our experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of regression-freedom checking in validating security patches while outlining the challenges that remain.

Abstract: Self-evolving personal agents act for users and learn from user feedback. However, if an agent obeys an instruction that an untrusted third party hides in content, the third party gains control of the agent. Such indirect prompt injection (IPI) poses severe risks. We investigate whether self-evolution degrades the robustness of personal agents to IPI. To study this question, we design a controlled two-phase methodology. In the adaptation phase, the user asks the agent to read external content and complete the task described in it, a common request in daily use. The agent then learns from the user's feedback and improves itself as designed. In the test phase, a third party writes a similar task into new external content, but the user's test request does not authorize the injected task. Using this methodology, we build multiple tasks with diverse test instances in different domains for a real personal agent. Our extensive experiments reveal that self-evolution substantially degrades robustness to IPI. Specifically, compared with the base agent, which does not evolve from user feedback, the average attack success rate rises by 14.0--48.8 percentage points under skill evolution and by 3.0--39.3 percentage points under reinforcement learning, posing safety risks. The code is available at https://github.com/SaFo-Lab/Evolve-IPI

Abstract: Customizing Pretrained Models (PMs) for downstream tasks demands task-specific data, which burgeoning Web3 applications provide abundantly. Blockchain-powered federated learning (BCFL) offers a secure and privacy-preserving paradigm to customize PMs among self-interested and untrusted Web3 participants. Yet, existing BCFL algorithms face severe efficiency and scalability challenges when applied to large PMs, due to their substantial computational and memory requirements and the hardware constraints of many participants. Theoretical analysis and extensive experimental results on customizing vision and language PMs demonstrate DICE's efficiency, effectiveness, and security.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents often receive access to more files, accounts, and services than a task requires. Prompt injection turns this excess access into an opportunity to redirect execution toward unrelated resources. Applying least privilege therefore requires identifying the assets needed by a task, including assets whose identities emerge only during execution. We present REALM (Required Execution Assets Linked to a Mission), an approach to identifying and maintaining a task's required assets and their permitted uses. The approach represents required assets as Dependency Graph nodes, resolves prerequisite relationships, and admits newly discovered assets through source-scoped selection. It separates discovering an asset from authorizing its use, while preserving the agent's freedom to choose its computation. The REALM Builder maintains the selected grants and projects Asset Files; the Partial API Manager exposes resource-bound Partial APIs to the Agent inside a Task Container. Across 60 clean tasks and 560 injected cases in AgentDyn, it completes 40.0% and 36.4% of tasks and decreases ASR from 16.6% to 0.2% with qwen3.5-flash as the base model.

Abstract: Recent latent-based watermarking methods can embed undetectable watermarks into AI-generated content. However, these approaches are vulnerable to removal attacks as their watermark detection boundaries are exposed, prompting the use of secret long-term transformations to hide these boundaries. We show that such defenses are insecure under a stronger threat model motivated by publicly reachable detectors: an adversary with batched detector access can reconstruct the transformations, permanently re-exposing the decision boundary. To address this, we propose a watermarking scheme based on a hybrid of long-term and one-time secret transformations, which prevents the reuse of structure across samples and keeps the long-term secret hidden. Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in automating complex information-seeking and transactional tasks across the open web. However, their real-world reliability remains limited by access barriers, such as dead links, authentication walls, rate limits, and anti-scraping defenses. These barriers block the primary execution path, causing agents to abort prematurely, loop indefinitely, or quietly compromise the user's original constraints. Existing evaluations either implicitly assume that target resources are freely accessible or focus on transient perturbations that simple retries can resolve, failing to capture scenarios where agents must actively reroute around impassable bottlenecks. To bridge this gap, this paper introduces AgentHeal, a comprehensive benchmark evaluating goal-preserving resource recovery, defined as the ability of web agents to detect non-transient access barriers, pivot to legitimate alternative sources, and fulfill the user's exact original objective. Spanning nine barrier families across a counterfactual sandbox and real-website replay, our experiments on leading LLM backbones show that average task success drops sharply from 89.4% to 43.7% under access barriers. Further analysis reveals that agents frequently fall for deceptive look-alikes, fail to escalate to users when necessary, violate access policies, or waste computation on futile retries, providing actionable diagnostics for developing resilient web agents.

</details>

Abstract: Classifying malware binaries rendered as images is a popular approach to malware family identification, and papers on the standard benchmarks for this task report accuracies of 97-99%. In this paper we audit ten of these benchmarks for near-duplicate images and test-train leakage, and retrain standard classifiers under naive and content-aware splits. Deduplicating by file hash, which is the standard practice, does not prevent leakage, because distinct binaries render to identical images. Deduplicating on image content reduces top-1 accuracy by up to 8 points and macro-F1 by up to 15 points on the released benchmarks we find leaky. The rendering step also adds duplication, since byteplots of the same binaries contain 19.7% near-duplicates where static feature vectors contain 2.2%, and changing the width at which bytes are wrapped into rows reduces accuracy by 20 to 33 points, although no byte changes. We recommend that content-level duplication and a non-image baseline be reported alongside accuracy.

Abstract: Although decades of research have yielded sophisticated mechanisms for enforcing memory safety, speculative execution can subvert these protections through transient memory operations. Conversely, defenses designed specifically for speculative safety, such as Speculative Load Hardening (SLH), cannot enforce memory safety during non-speculative execution. A naive combination of defenses from both domains raises two fundamental challenges: (1) which combination of otherwise fragmented defenses can ensure dual memory safety under speculative and non-speculative executions? (2) whether such combinations introduce redundant protections that incur unnecessary performance overhead?
  We present DualGuard, a unified framework for dual memory protection under non-speculative and speculative executions. DualGuard systematically analyzes memory behaviors, classifying them into four categories, and further identifies the minimal composition requirements for dual memory safety, which allow us to strategically combine existing mitigations and design new ones to avoid redundant or excessive protections. We implement DualGuard through a combination of static analysis and runtime enforcement within the LLVM infrastructure. Our evaluation demonstrates that DualGuard reduces overhead by 3%-72% compared to naive compositions of fragmented defenses. In some cases, it even incurs lower overhead than approaches that guarantee memory safety solely under either non-speculative or speculative execution.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems increasingly rely on persistent memory and inter-agent communication. These capabilities create privacy risks that extend beyond individual interactions: a release appropriate in isolation may become inappropriate when combined with information previously disclosed to a recipient or propagated to downstream agents along the planned workflow. Existing Contextual Integrity (CI) mechanisms for agentic systems typically rely on local judgments that assess each disclosure in isolation, with or without regard to the recipient's history, but do not account for how information propagates downstream. However, compliance at the current boundary does not guarantee appropriateness across the workflow. We introduce Task-Graph Contextual Integrity (TG-CI), to our knowledge the first task-graph-native formalization of Contextual Integrity for multi-agent systems. TG-CI represents a workflow as a Multi-Agent Task Graph (MATG) and extends CI from atomic transfers to stateful, graph-level information flows by jointly considering an item's intended propagation scope and recipients' evolving disclosure states. At comparable accuracy levels, neuron-threshold perturbations increase reconstruction fidelity, measured by SSIM, by up to 51.8%, whereas spike-level perturbations primarily degrade utility without consistently improving reconstruction. Weight noise, in contrast, reduces leakage under the same inversion procedure. These results show that hardware variability is not inherently privacy-preserving: model inversion leakage in memristive SNNs depends on the hardware state and cannot be inferred from model accuracy alone.

Abstract: Large language model serving stacks, commercial inference APIs, vector databases, and agent orchestration gateways are now ex- posed on the public internet and indexed by commodity scanners within hours of deployment. The threat characteristics of this sur- face are only beginning to be measured, and the honeypot frame- works that could measure it were designed for conventional net- work services rather than for AI infrastructure. In fact, emulating an AI service is a distinct deception problem: the observable behavior of the service, including model inventories, token formats, tool-call protocols, and authorization flows, must be reproduced faithfully, while the underlying computation, model inference and tool ex- ecution, must never occur. We present InferD, a low-interaction honeypot framework organized around a single principle we call deception without inference: every request an adversary can observe is answered from static response pools, and nothing an adversary submits is ever executed. InferD decomposes the emulation into four deception planes, transport, service protocol, vulnerability surface, and agentic session, adds a provenance plane that links a planted credential to evidence of downstream model-mediated processing.

Abstract: We present a novel post-quantum cryptographic framework based on masked high-memory convolutional codes in the Niederreiter paradigm. The public key is derived from a high-memory polynomial parity-check matrix augmented by dense random masking and additional invertible linear transformations. The resulting matrix exhibits strong random-like properties, effectively concealing its algebraic structure and resisting known structural and algebraic attacks. Legitimate recipients retain polynomial-time decoding capability, whereas adversaries appear to be limited to generic information-set decoding with exponential complexity. Under standard cryptanalytic estimates, the proposed construction achieves security margins exceeding those of classical McEliece- and Niederreiter-based systems by factors greater than 2^200. Beyond its enhanced security profile, the construction offers considerable design flexibility, supporting arbitrary plaintext lengths, linear-time decryption, and a uniform per-bit computational cost, enabling efficient scalability to very long messages. Practical implementation is facilitated by a parallel array of syndrome-trellis decoders, dynamically instantiated according to candidate syndrome values, enabling efficient hardware and software implementations with high throughput. Overall, the proposed scheme is a promising candidate for robust, scalable, quantum-resistant public-key cryptography.

</details>

Abstract: Deterministic variants of CSIDH remove randomness from the group action, either through implementation techniques or, as in Houben's deterministic CSIDH and OSIDH-LD, by design. Since earlier fault attacks on CSIDH mostly target random point sampling, determinism seems to close that avenue. We show that it opens another: every secret-dependent step occurs at a fixed, public point of every execution, so an attacker can fault the same step repeatedly. Our attack forces the mask that selects which of two lanes computes the next isogeny to zero, for example by skipping one instruction. Whether the output changes reveals whether a secret exponent reaches a chosen threshold, and a binary search over these thresholds recovers the full key with one fault per execution: at most 297 oracle queries for OSIDH-LD and 222 for deterministic CSIDH with CSIDH-512-sized keys. We recover full OSIDH-LD keys in a software fault simulation of the reference implementation, and show on a ChipWhisperer-Lite that the targeted instruction is skipped with probability at least 0.94 at an isolated fault position. Finally, we argue that fault resistance requires giving up determinism, and propose countermeasures relying on randomization.

</details>

Abstract: Watermarking is essential for image copyright protection and attribution, yet existing methods suffer from fundamental trade-offs among post-hoc applicability to pre-existing images, multi-bit capacity, training-free deployment, and adversarial resilience. Abstract: Despite growing capabilities, LLMs remain susceptible to prompt injections: malicious inputs that can manipulate or hijack models. In particular, it has recently been observed that LLMs can exhibit role confusion (Ye et al., 2026), where models attribute the source of text based on its style instead of its labeled role. Malicious text in tool outputs (for instance, a malicious webpage accessed by an agent) can leverage role confusion to impersonate the user, an attack we call role spoofing}. In this work, we seek to understand and mitigate role spoofing. We study how models track "who said what" in a conversation, showing that they use cues from both content (e.g., semantics and writing style) and structure (e.g., control tokens and formatting). We show that LLMs are highly susceptible to role spoofing attacks that combine both fake structure and content. Additionally, in a toy setting, we show that adversarial training which teaches the model to ignore content-based injections does mitigate content-based attacks but leaves the model susceptible to structure-based attacks. This can explain why frontier models, with increased resistance to many injection attacks, can still be susceptible to role spoofing. Finally, we propose a new method for detecting role spoofing using linear probes trained to predict roles in a conversation, and show that it is effective across different settings.

Abstract: This research proposes a two-level keystroke dynamics authentication system that provides defense-in-depth security through continuous behavioral drift monitoring. Level 1 employs fixed-text password authentication using Temporal Convolutional Networks (TCN) with contrastive learning and population-based training (PBT), achieving 3.77% EER for high-security login verification. Upon successful authentication, Level 2 continuously monitors user typing during natural free-text sessions using a 4-Model Deep Learning Ensemble. If suspicious behavioral drift is detected during monitoring, the system re-authenticates the user by returning to Level 1, providing a feedback loop against session hijacking and behavioral anomalies. The system explicitly models long-term behavioral drift through the fixed-text component's PBT mechanism, which adapts to temporal variations in password typing patterns. This research leverages free-text (Buffalo dataset) and fixed-text (GREYC dataset) keystroke dynamics datasets, extracting temporal and spatial features including hold times, up-down latencies, down-down intervals, and trigram patterns. The final system achieves 96%+ accuracy for fixed-text authentication (3.77% EER) and 83.53% True Acceptance Rate (TAR) at 5% False Acceptance Rate (FAR) for free-text continuous authentication (10.05% EER), demonstrating superior performance and strong resistance to impostor attacks through continuous behavioral monitoring.

Abstract: RecHash is a stateful sequential authentication construction based on paired hash-chain openings and an authenticated record history. It develops the decreasing position relation of the 2019 hash-entangled construction, whose exact integer normalization yields a two-coordinate constant-sum antichain. Direct proportional reuse is constrained by integer capacity and multiplicative contraction. The main construction instead assigns each record to a predetermined chain segment, with complementary positions encoding each digest block. All digests fit every scheduled slot, independently of preceding messages. For an $\ell$-bit digest distributed over $k$ equal-length chain pairs, balanced allocation gives $\lfloor L/(2^{\lceil\ell/k\rceil}+1)\rfloor$ slots. A 256-bit digest, 32 pairs, and length 65535 provide 254 application records followed by a renewal certificate, with 2048 bytes of openings per record for 256-bit chain outputs. We give a complete segmented state transition, a worked trace, exact allocation and resource results, and a renewal-free security-composition theorem conditional on authenticated history and an adaptive one-step guarantee. The analysis relates the construction to constant-sum encodings and standardized hash-based signatures; a concrete post-quantum reduction and security across renewal remain open.

Abstract: Advanced power adjusting block withholding attacks such as PAW and T-PAW have been analyzed so far under a global-reset version of the proportional scheme (GRP), which is rarely used in practice. In this paper, we analyze T-PAW, which contains FAW and PAW as special cases, under the block-dependent payout schemes deployed in practice: Pay-Per-Last-N-Shares (PPLNS), round-based Proportional (PROP) and exponential-decay (EXP). We derive the revenue ratios analytically, by renewal arguments for PPLNS and through the stationary carry process of an open round for PROP and EXP. We further introduce Power Adjusting Hopping (PAH), where an adversary of any size adjusts its power allocation towards the pool according to the blockwork age of the round without withholding any block, and derive its revenue ratios under PROP and EXP. We show that GRP misestimates the T-PAW gain in a scheme-dependent direction: it overstates the gain under PROP up to an order of magnitude and understates it under EXP for the original slush decay rate. PROP is highly vulnerable to PAH, while EXP faces a trade-off: a slow decay invites PAH and a fast decay invites T-PAW. PPLNS is the least vulnerable scheme: it is immune to PAH and it mitigates, but does not eliminate, T-PAW. All analytical results are verified by Monte Carlo simulations.

Abstract: We present TextSeal2, a distortion-free watermark for large language models that keeps its signal under speculative decoding: tokens that replace rejected drafts remain watermarked with the same keys, and the acceptance rate is unchanged. It also preserves output diversity and adds no measurable inference latency. Its predecessor, TextSeal, restores diversity in the Gumbel-max scheme by routing each token to one of two keys via a coin flip. This is suboptimal because the detector averages the scores from both keys without knowing which key was used. TextSeal2 instead merges two or more keys into a single virtual key and couples it to fresh noise through AutoMark's Gaussian copula. Any number of keys, including publicly shared keys, can be merged this way without diluting detection. Under speculative decoding, TextSeal2 verifies keyed drafts with one more exponential race, run on the leftovers of the draft races. By memorylessness, this race reproduces block verification, so the output follows the target model exactly. We evaluate TextSeal2 on three LLMs. At equal output token diversity, TextSeal2 detects 8 to 22 percentage points more 100-token answers than TextSeal at a false-positive rate of $10^{-3}$, and is comparable to textGrain while requiring no post-logits optimization. Under speculative decoding, it detects 18 and 22 percentage points more 200-token answers than TextSeal and textGrain, respectively, at the same acceptance rate.
